```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \S\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).
5	
6	\subsection{Main Results}
7	\label{sec:main_results}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$N$} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} \\
17	\midrule
18	Vanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\pm$3.3 \\
19	TaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\pm$8.6 \\
20	Random  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\pm$7.0 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \%). TaxEI vs.\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total, confirming injection itself---not data volume---drives the effect.}
24	\label{tab:main_results}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	Variance is dose-dependent and non-linear. At full training scale (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), but this ordering is not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}) reveals the opposite at moderate injection: at 25\%, TaxEI SD (1.2pp) falls \emph{below} vanilla---injection deterministically harms all seeds. At 50\%, variance explodes for both methods (TaxEI 12.4pp, Random 11.7pp), far exceeding vanilla. This suggests two distinct injection regimes: deterministic degradation at moderate doses, and chaotic seed-dependent outcomes at high doses.
28	
29	\subsection{Dose-Response}
30	\label{sec:dose_response}
31	
32	To control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\% and 50\% reveal a dual regime rather than a simple threshold.
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\small
37	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
38	\toprule
39	\textbf{Dose} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{TaxEI}} \\
42	10\%$^\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\
43	25\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\
44	50\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Random}} \\
47	10\%$^\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\
48	25\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\
49	50\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\Delta$: mean change vs.\ vanilla (65.3$\pm$3.3). $^\dagger$Single seed. At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\%, variance explodes (SD${\approx}$12pp), with individual seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\%.}
53	\label{tab:dose_response}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}
59	\caption{Dose-response: accuracy vs.\ injection ratio (seed=42 only). Multi-seed means and standard deviations in Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}.}
60	\label{fig:dose_response}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	At moderate injection (25\%), TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\sim$47\% ($-17.8$pp vs.\ vanilla, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp) with higher variance (SD${=}$5.3pp). At high injection (50\%), both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (TaxEI SD${=}$12.4pp, Random SD${=}$11.7pp), with individual seeds ranging from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds (max 68.1\%). The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\% TaxEI mean (56.0\%) exceeds 25\% mean (47.5\%), suggesting high-dose injection can occasionally unlock performance through stochastic exploration.
64	
65	At 10\% injection (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla seed=42 exactly (61.7\%), while Random achieves 68.1\%---within vanilla seed range (61.7--68.1\%).
66	
67	\subsection{Task-Type Vulnerability}
68	\label{sec:task_vulnerability}
69	
70	The TaxEI--Random gap is not uniform across task types. Three-seed averages reveal that two of six task types drive the aggregate difference: \texttt{pick\_cool} (TaxEI$-$Random $= -19.0$pp) and \texttt{pick\_two\_obj} ($-14.3$pp). The remaining four types show gaps $\leq$4.2pp, suggesting taxonomy-guided errors interact with specific action-sequence structures rather than causing uniform degradation.
71	
72	\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}
73	\label{sec:recovery}
74	
75	We test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\% recovery (vs.\ Vanilla 61.7\%, Random 63.8\%)---no statistically significant improvement.
76	
77	The uniform 0\% detection rate across all conditions suggests that taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.
78	
79	\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}
80	\label{sec:operator_split}
81	
82	Figure~\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery performance by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$ per operator). The results suggest a hypothesis-generating observation: local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step order swap: 7/12 vs.\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial, while global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful.
83	
84	\begin{figure}[t]
85	\centering
86	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}
87	\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$ per operator; $\pm1$ task $\approx$ $\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}
88	\label{fig:operator_gradient}
89	\end{figure}
90	
91	Local errors leave the plan's causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. C1 (context truncation), though a Context-type operator, behaves globally: truncated history forces all subsequent decisions to rely on incomplete information. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$); we cannot draw statistically robust conclusions.
92	
93	\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}
94	\label{sec:paradox}
95	
96	At $\geq$25\% injection, models can achieve \emph{lower} training loss but \emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\% (vs.\ 61.7\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: (1)~memorization of noise \cite{arpit2017memorization}---the model memorizes injected patterns as surface sequences \cite{zhou2023lima} without generalizing; and (2)~distribution shift---at $\geq$25\%, the training distribution diverges sufficiently from the clean evaluation distribution that generalization degrades despite lower loss. The extreme seed variance at 50\% (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) further complicates this picture: some seeds (e.g., TaxEI-50\% seed=0, 70.2\%) escape degradation entirely, suggesting the paradox is seed-dependent at high injection.
97

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). Multi-seed results in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n"
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total, confirming injection itself---not data volume---drives the effect.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nVariance is dose-dependent and non-linear. At full training scale (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), but this ordering is not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) reveals the opposite at moderate injection: at 25\\%, TaxEI SD (1.2pp) falls \\emph{below} vanilla---injection deterministically harms all seeds. At 50\\%, variance explodes for both methods (TaxEI 12.4pp, Random 11.7pp), far exceeding vanilla. This suggests two distinct injection regimes: deterministic degradation at moderate doses, and chaotic seed-dependent outcomes at high doses.\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime rather than a simple threshold.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with individual seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response: accuracy vs.\\ injection ratio (seed=42 only). Multi-seed means and standard deviations in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt moderate injection (25\\%), TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp vs.\\ vanilla, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp) with higher variance (SD${=}$5.3pp). At high injection (50\\%), both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (TaxEI SD${=}$12.4pp, Random SD${=}$11.7pp), with individual seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds (max 68.1\\%). The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%), suggesting high-dose injection can occasionally unlock performance through stochastic exploration.\n\nAt 10\\% injection (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla seed=42 exactly (61.7\\%), while Random achieves 68.1\\%---within vanilla seed range (61.7--68.1\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Task-Type Vulnerability}\n\\label{sec:task_vulnerability}\n\nThe TaxEI--Random gap is not uniform across task types. Three-seed averages reveal that two of six task types drive the aggregate difference: \\texttt{pick\\_cool} (TaxEI$-$Random $= -19.0$pp) and \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj} ($-14.3$pp). The remaining four types show gaps $\\leq$4.2pp, suggesting taxonomy-guided errors interact with specific action-sequence structures rather than causing uniform degradation.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement.\n\nThe uniform 0\\% detection rate across all conditions suggests that taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery performance by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$ per operator). The results suggest a hypothesis-generating observation: local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step order swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial, while global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$ per operator; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\nLocal errors leave the plan's causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. C1 (context truncation), though a Context-type operator, behaves globally: truncated history forces all subsequent decisions to rely on incomplete information. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$); we cannot draw statistically robust conclusions.\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: (1)~memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization}---the model memorizes injected patterns as surface sequences \\cite{zhou2023lima} without generalizing; and (2)~distribution shift---at $\\geq$25\\%, the training distribution diverges sufficiently from the clean evaluation distribution that generalization degrades despite lower loss. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) further complicates this picture: some seeds (e.g., TaxEI-50\\% seed=0, 70.2\\%) escape degradation entirely, suggesting the paradox is seed-dependent at high injection.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}\nWe hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}\nPALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing an estimated $\\sim$30pp of its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}\nAt 50\\% injection, error injection creates a training lottery: individual seeds produce outcomes ranging from well below vanilla (46.8\\%) to above the best vanilla seed (70.2\\%). The 70.2\\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting it is a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. The practical implication is that practitioners cannot predict in advance which outcome they will get. This cross-condition coincidence---two different methods producing identical outlier performance at different seeds---may reflect a specific seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction that warrants further investigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power. The dose-response ablation at 10\\% uses a single seed; 25\\% and 50\\% are confirmed across 3 seeds. TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation partially controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. We acknowledge the tension in arguing $n{=}3$ is insufficient while reporting only $n{=}3$. ALFWorld uses a text-based interface where errors produce discrete feedback (``Nothing happens''), partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch — D023 叙事重构] 多 seed dose-response 数据已全部到齐，需要对论文做重大叙事重构。以下是完整数据和 10 项修改指令。

## 完整多 seed 数据

| Condition | Mean±SD | s42 | s0 | s123 |
|-----------|---------|-----|-----|------|
| Vanilla | 65.3±3.3 | 61.7 | 68.1 | 66.0 |
| TaxEI full | 56.7±8.6 | 61.7 | 46.8 | 61.7 |
| Random full | 63.1±7.0 | 66.0 | 55.3 | 68.1 |
| TaxEI 25% | 47.5±1.2 | 48.9 | 46.8 | 46.8 |
| TaxEI 50% | 56.0±12.4 | 51.1 | 70.2 | 46.8 |
| Random 25% | 56.0±5.3 | 55.3 | 61.7 | 51.1 |
| Random 50% | 58.1±11.7 | 57.4 | 46.8 | 70.2 |

## 10 项修改

### 1. Table 2 (dose_response) 更新为 3-seed
替换当前的单 seed Table 2。新表格需要包含 25% 和 50% 两个剂量的 3-seed 均值±SD。10% 仅有 seed=42，保留单 seed 标注。格式：

| Dose | TaxEI Mean±SD | Random Mean±SD |
|------|--------------|----------------|
| 0% (Vanilla) | 65.3±3.3 | 65.3±3.3 |
| 10% | 61.7† | 68.1† |
| 25% | 47.5±1.2 | 56.0±5.3 |
| 50% | 56.0±12.4 | 58.1±11.7 |

†seed=42 only

### 2. Contribution 2 重写为 "dual regime"
当前 Contribution 2 是 "dose-response threshold"。重写为：发现两个截然不同的剂量区间——25% injection 产生 deterministic harm（TaxEI SD=1.2pp），50% injection 产生 training lottery（TaxEI SD=12.4pp，个体 seed 从 46.8% 到 70.2%）。

### 3. 删除所有 "shared 12.8pp threshold" 和 "plateau" 引用
多 seed 数据表明：(a) TaxEI 25% drop = 17.8pp (from vanilla mean)，Random 25% drop = 9.3pp——幅度不共享；(b) 50% mean > 25% mean（非单调），所以没有 plateau。搜索全文删除所有 "shared threshold" / "12.8pp" / "plateau" 的表述。

### 4. Discussion 引入 "training lottery" 概念
新增一段：在 50% injection 下，训练结果变成 lottery——seed=0 在所有其他条件下都是最差 seed（full-scale 46.8%, 25% 46.8%），但在 50% injection 下突然变成 70.2%（超过所有 vanilla seed）。实践者无法事前预测哪个 seed 会受益或受害。这比 "系统性有害" 更有实践价值：error injection 的问题是 unreliability，不是 consistent harm。

### 5. Variance narrative 按剂量分层
当前的 variance narrative 是全局递增（Vanilla < Random < TaxEI）。修正为按剂量分层：
- 25%: TaxEI SD=1.2pp（deterministic harm，方差极低）
- 50%: TaxEI SD=12.4pp, Random SD=11.7pp（training lottery，方差极高）
- Full-scale: TaxEI SD=8.6pp, Random SD=7.0pp（中等方差）
关键洞察：高剂量不是 "更有害"，而是 "更不可预测"。

### 6. Introduction Contribution 2 更新
Introduction 中列出的 Contribution 2 对应更新为 dual regime 表述。

### 7. Abstract 更新
Abstract 中删除 "shared dose-response threshold at 10–25%" 和 "plateau"，替换为 dual regime 发现：25% injection 产生一致退化（TaxEI -17.8pp, SD=1.2pp across 3 seeds），50% 产生 training lottery（SD=12.4pp，个体 seed 跨越 46.8%-70.2%）。

### 8. Fig 1 caption 更新
如果不重画图（单 seed 图），在 caption 中标注 "seed=42 only; 3-seed data in Table 2 reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25%→50%"。

### 9. Conclusion 修改
Conclusion 中的 practical guidelines 更新为反映 dual regime 发现。第 4 条 recovery pairing criterion 保留。

### 10. 70.2% 跨条件一致性讨论
Discussion 或 Analysis 中加一句：TaxEI 50% s0 和 Random 50% s123 都精确达到 70.2%（47 tasks 中 33 个正确），表明这个异常值不是 taxonomy-specific 现象，而是高剂量 injection 的通用效应——在某些 seed-data 组合下，错误注入反而提供了有益的数据多样性。

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). Multi-seed results in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",

_source line 1226 · 2026-05-15T11:14:08.925Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director (supervisor) has been instructing the Paper Agent to execute multiple rounds of modifications to a research paper titled "When Does Unpaired Error Injection Help LLM Agents? Boundary Conditions from a Failed Transfer to Embodied Planning" targeting "Insights from Negative Results in NLP @ EMNLP 2026". The session covers four major modification rounds:
   
   **Round A**: Review Director Round 1 Action Matrix — 17 items including claims restructuring (3 boundary conditions → 2 contributions + observations), recovery-pairing confound framing, variance gradient downgrade, data volume confound, page compression, PALADIN attribution, per-operator exact counts, AgentDebug comparison, Reflexion citation, text-based limitation, v3→v4 discrepancy expansion, and "no statistically significant improvement" consistency.
   
   **Round B**: Reviewer残留修正 — 3 items: Abstract "identify"→"observe", Conclusion 4th criterion (recovery pairing), Introduction "no statistically significant benefit". Plus Version A/B preparation for post-multi-seed switching.
   
   **Round C**: Pending version preparation — Draft paragraphs for bifurcation hypothesis, dose-response supplement, Table 2 template, threshold wording 3 levels. Saved as drafts, no paper modification.
   
   **Round D (D023)**: Major narrative restructuring with complete multi-seed dose-response data. 10 items: Table 2 3-seed, Contribution 2 "dual regime", delete "shared 12.8pp"/"plateau", training lottery concept, dose-dependent variance, Abstract/Intro/Conclusion updates, Fig 1 caption, 70.2% cross-condition consistency.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Taxonomy-guided error injection (TaxEI) with 4 operators: P1 (step_order_swap), P2 (sub_goal_deletion), C1 (context_truncation), C4 (observation_masking)
   - Three training conditions: Vanilla (4,358 samples), TaxEI (9,029), Random (9,029)
   - Qwen3-8B LoRA SFT, rank=16, α=32, lr=1e-4, 3 epochs, bf16, 3 seeds (42/0/123)
   - ALFWorld benchmark: 47 test tasks, 6 types
   - **Dual regime discovery**: 25% injection = deterministic degradation (TaxEI SD=1.2pp), 50% = training lottery (SD≈12pp)
   - **Non-monotonic dose-response**: 50% TaxEI mean (56.0%) > 25% mean (47.5%)
   - **70.2% anomaly**: Occurs in both TaxEI s0 and Random s123 at 50% — generic high-dose phenomenon
   - Recovery-pairing confound: PALADIN pairs errors with recovery demos, TaxEI doesn't — two competing hypotheses
   - Per-operator local/global split: C4(+9.1pp), P1(0.0pp), C1(-8.3pp), P2(-8.3pp) — exploratory, N=11-12
   - All main comparisons NS: TaxEI vs Vanilla p=0.054
   - ACL 2023 LaTeX template with \shortcite support

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/main.tex`
     - Master document. Title changed to "When Does Unpaired Error Injection Help LLM Agents?"
     - Uses ACL 2023 style with review option, amsmath, booktabs, enumitem, amssymb, pifont
     - \input order: abstract, introduction, method, experiments, analysis (empty), discussion, related_work, conclusion, bibliography, appendix

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/abstract.tex`
     - Final version: "+57pp system-level improvement (combining taxonomy-guided injection, recovery pairing, and inference-time retrieval)"
     - Recovery-pairing confound: "PALADIN pairs errors with recovery demonstrations, while our TaxEI injects standalone errors"
     - Dual regime: "at moderate injection (25%), performance degrades deterministically (-17.8pp, SD=1.2pp, 3 seeds), while at high injection (50%), extreme seed sensitivity emerges (SD≈12pp)"
     - Per-operator split as additional observation

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/introduction.tex`
     - Two numbered contributions: (1) Negative transfer with competing hypotheses, (2) Dose-dependent dual regime
     - Contribution 2 includes: non-monotonic finding (50% mean 56.0% > 25% mean 47.5%)
     - Additional observations: per-operator split, training-eval paradox
     - "no statistically significant benefit" wording

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/experiments.tex`
     - §4.1 Main Results: Task-type vulnerability folded into one sentence. Variance paragraph shortened to 2 sentences with forward reference to dose-response.
     - §4.2 Dose-Response: New 3-seed Table 2 with per-seed columns (s42/s0/s123/Mean±SD/Δ). TaxEI and Random sections. Dual regime narrative. Non-monotonic finding. Fig 1 caption: "seed=42 only. 3-seed data in Table 2 reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25%→50%."
     - §4.3 Task-Type Vulnerability: REMOVED (merged into §4.1)
     - §4.4 Recovery: Merged to one paragraph. 0% detection as standalone observation.
     - §4.5 Per-Operator: Compressed. Exact task counts "7/11 vs 6/11" format. "hypothesis-generating observation".
     - §4.6 Training-Eval Paradox: Updated for seed-dependent nature at 50%. References 70.2% anomaly.

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/discussion.tex`
     - §5.1 Structural hypothesis: Includes Reflexion citation
     - §5.2 Recovery-pairing confound: "We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses"
     - §5.3 Training lottery (NEW): 70.2% in TaxEI s0 and Random s123, seed-initialization × injection interaction
     - §5.4 Limitations: Text-based interface caveat (SF-7), augmentation comparison caveat (SF-8), dose-response 10% single-seed/25-50% 3-seed confirmed

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/related_work.tex`
     - 3 paragraphs (compressed from 4): Error injection agents (with AgentDebug 3-sentence comparison), Training dynamics and data mixing (merged), ALFWorld methods
     - "dose-dependent variance findings" replaces "directional variance pattern"
     - "No prior work has tested training-time error injection on ALFWorld" added

   - `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/conclusion.tex`
     - Dual regime (3-seed confirmed at 25%/50%)

     - Unchanged in this session. Contains task environment, error taxonomy, training setup.

   - `pending_patches.md` — Created then DELETED (obsoleted by D023 data)

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **7-page overflow after D023 restructuring**: After adding dual regime narrative, training lottery paragraph, expanded Table 2, and dose-dependent variance, the paper grew from 6 to 7 pages (body 5 instead of 4). Fixed by: (a) folding §4.3 task-type vulnerability into one sentence in §4.1, (b) shortening §4.1 variance to 2 sentences, (c) compressing §4.2 narrative, (d) shortening Discussion training lottery to 3 sentences, (e) trimming Limitations (removed "We acknowledge the tension in arguing n=3..." sentence), (f) compressing training-eval paradox.
   - **Redundant dose-response sentences in Limitations**: After Round A, had "The dose-response ablation uses a single seed; multi-seed replication is underway. Dose-response ablation used seed=42 due to chronological experimental design." Combined into single sentence.
   - **\shortcite false positives in consistency checker**: check_paper_consistency reports 6 "orphan_bib_key" warnings for entries cited via \shortcite (checker doesn't detect \shortcite). Confirmed as false positives — compilation resolves all references correctly.
   - **Appendix "12.8pp" false alarm**: Grep for "12.8pp" found one instance in appendix, but it refers to v3→v4 version discrepancy (74.5%→61.7%), not the old dose-response threshold. Correctly left in place.

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\\%$\\to$50\\%.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",
        "numLines": 86,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 86
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/experiments.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "9385e30b-8b9a-41db-bab5-aeabeb6f94c0",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}\nWe hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}\nPALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing an estimated $\\sim$30pp of its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}\nAt 50\\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld uses a text-based interface where errors produce discrete feedback (``Nothing happens''), partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/discussion.tex"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "35ad1bfd-cb22-4b13-92dd-e8b161313953",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Error injection for LLM agents.}\nSeveral methods augment agent training with failure trajectories: PALADIN \\cite{paladin2025} uses taxonomy-guided injection with retrieval-augmented recovery; ETO \\cite{song2024eto} applies DPO on exploration failures; EEF \\cite{eef2025} extracts positive signals from expert failures; Wang et al.\\ \\shortcite{wang2024learning_from_failure} and AgentHER \\cite{agentHER2025} integrate contrastive or relabeled negatives. AgentDebug \\cite{agentdebug2025} identifies five error dimensions including error cascading---a phenomenon our per-operator analysis independently corroborates. Their diagnostic taxonomy differs from our injection-oriented 4-operator design: AgentDebug classifies observed errors post-hoc, while our operators generate controlled perturbations. The overlap in cascading-error findings suggests this structural property is a robust moderator of error-injection effectiveness. Our work tests \\emph{cross-domain transfer} of the injection paradigm using direct SFT mixing.\n\n\\paragraph{Training dynamics and data mixing.}\nMcCoy et al.\\ \\shortcite{mccoy2020seeds} and Mosbach et al.\\ \\shortcite{mosbach2021stability} document seed sensitivity in fine-tuning; D'Amour et al.\\ \\shortcite{damour2022underspecification} frame this as underspecification. Our dose-dependent variance findings extend these observations to error-augmented agent training. Rolnick et al.\\ \\shortcite{rolnick2017robust} show robustness to massive label noise in vision---contrasting with our sharp degradation at 25\\% injection in sequential decision-making. Ye et al.\\ \\shortcite{ye2025mixing_laws} and Bengio et al.\\ \\shortcite{bengio2009curriculum} establish that data composition has non-linear effects on SFT performance.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld methods.}\nReAct \\cite{yao2023react} establishes think/act prompting. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% via multi-trial inference-time self-reflection---notably, inference-time reflection succeeds where our training-time injection fails. CoEx \\cite{kim2025coex} reaches 93.3\\% with co-evolving world models; AgentPRM \\cite{agentprm2025} achieves 91.0\\% with process reward models. Our 65.3\\% vanilla baseline is consistent with single-trial SFT on an 8B model. No prior work has tested training-time error injection on ALFWorld.\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "328ee49d-11d3-4bc8-9e01-3ee4cdb406ef",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe tested whether unpaired taxonomy-guided error injection---effective for tool-use agents---transfers to embodied planning. On ALFWorld, TaxEI provides no statistically significant improvement over clean training ($-8.6$pp, $p{=}0.054$). Two competing hypotheses explain this: embodied planning may lack the discrete, observable error structure required for injection to help, or the absence of recovery pairing (present in PALADIN) may render standalone errors unlearnable. We additionally identify a dose-dependent dual regime (3-seed confirmed at 25\\%/50\\%): deterministic degradation at moderate injection, chaotic seed-dependent outcomes at high injection. We also observe a per-operator local/global split (exploratory, $N{=}11$--$12$).\n\nBased on our results and the two competing hypotheses, we suggest that practitioners considering error injection for embodied tasks check four properties: (1)~are errors discrete and observable? (2)~can recovery be achieved locally? (3)~does the task provide immediate feedback? (4)~are errors paired with explicit recovery demonstrations? These criteria require validation in other environments.\n\nFuture work should first test recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld to disentangle the task-structure hypothesis from recovery-pairing effects, then replicate on a second environment (e.g., WebShop) and investigate the seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction observed at high doses.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

{
  "parentUuid": "8e7aad02-aef9-44c2-b0f8-d0bd888f976b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTraining LLM agents on failure trajectories is an increasingly popular paradigm. PALADIN \\cite{paladin2025} demonstrates that taxonomy-guided error injection---where training data includes systematically corrupted trajectories tagged with error types---yields +57pp system-level improvement for tool-use agents (combining taxonomy-guided injection, recovery pairing, and inference-time retrieval). Related approaches show gains across tool-use \\cite{song2024eto,eef2025,wang2024learning_from_failure} and dialogue tasks \\cite{agentHER2025}. A natural question arises: does this transfer to embodied planning, where agents must execute multi-step physical manipulation tasks?\n\nWe test this on ALFWorld \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld} with TaxEI, a four-operator error taxonomy for embodied planning, training Qwen3-8B agents under three conditions (Vanilla, TaxEI, Random) across three seeds. TaxEI yields no statistically significant improvement ($-8.6$pp, $p{=}0.054$) and directionally increases instability. Crucially, this comparison is confounded: PALADIN pairs each injected error with an explicit recovery demonstration, while our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. The negative result may therefore reflect missing recovery pairing rather than---or in addition to---differences in task structure.\n\nBeyond reporting this negative result, we make two contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Negative transfer with competing hypotheses.} Unpaired error injection provides no statistically significant benefit on ALFWorld. Two equally plausible explanations exist: (a)~embodied planning lacks the discrete, observable error structure that makes injection effective in tool-use, and (b)~the absence of recovery pairing renders injected errors unlearnable. Disentangling these requires recovery-paired injection experiments.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Dose-dependent dual regime.} At moderate injection (25\\%), performance degrades deterministically ($-17.8$pp vs.\\ vanilla, SD${=}$1.2pp, 3 seeds). At high injection (50\\%), extreme seed sensitivity emerges (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with individual seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nWe additionally observe a per-operator local/global split where local errors are learnable but global errors are harmful (exploratory, $N{=}11$--$12$), and a training-evaluation paradox at $\\geq$25\\% injection. These exploratory patterns offer preliminary guidance for ALFWorld-like embodied tasks.\n",
        "numLines": 17,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 17
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions.     "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CQ36k5drGFpJxebFR1QUTa`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Environment}
5	\label{sec:task_env}
6	
7	ALFWorld \cite{shridhar2021alfworld} is a text-based embodied environment where agents execute multi-step household tasks (e.g., \emph{heat a potato and place it in the fridge}). Each task requires a sequence of \texttt{think}/\texttt{act} steps in a ReAct-style loop \cite{yao2023react}. We use the standard 47-task unseen test split across six task types: \texttt{look\_at\_obj}, \texttt{pick\_and\_place}, \texttt{pick\_clean}, \texttt{pick\_cool}, \texttt{pick\_heat}, and \texttt{pick\_two\_obj}.
8	
9	\subsection{Error Taxonomy}
10	\label{sec:error_taxonomy}
11	
12	We define four error injection operators adapted for embodied planning, organized along two axes:
13	
14	\paragraph{Local operators} affect single steps without disrupting the overall plan structure:
15	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
16	\item \textbf{C4 (observation\_masking)}: Replaces environment observations with generic placeholders, removing perceptual grounding for one step.
17	\item \textbf{P1 (step\_order\_swap)}: Swaps two adjacent action steps, creating a locally incorrect but recoverable sequence.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\paragraph{Global operators} disrupt the trajectory's causal chain:
21	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
22	\item \textbf{P2 (sub\_goal\_deletion)}: Removes a complete sub-goal (e.g., the ``clean'' step in a pick-clean-place task), breaking the plan's logical structure.
23	\item \textbf{C1 (context\_truncation)}: Truncates the context window, causing all subsequent decisions to rely on incomplete history.
24	\end{itemize}
25	
26	All operators achieve 100\% injection legality (valid action format) and 97.5\% recovery quality (trajectories remain parseable). Error-recovery pairs are \emph{not} explicitly paired in training data---errors are mixed as additional demonstrations.
27	
28	\subsection{Training Setup}
29	\label{sec:training_setup}
30	
31	We collect 501 expert trajectories from GPT-5.2 on ALFWorld training tasks, yielding 4{,}358 clean SFT demonstrations. Error injection produces 4{,}671 additional trajectories (round-robin across the four operators).
32	
33	\paragraph{Three conditions:}
34	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
35	\item \textbf{Vanilla}: 4{,}358 clean demonstrations only.
36	\item \textbf{TaxEI}: 4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged errors (error type labels in prompt) = 9{,}029 total.
37	\item \textbf{Random}: 4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors (no taxonomy) = 9{,}029 total.
38	\end{itemize}
39	
40	All models fine-tune Qwen3-8B using LoRA (rank 16, $\alpha{=}32$, lr $1{\times}10^{-4}$, 3 epochs, bf16). Each condition is trained with three seeds (42, 0, 123).
41	
42	\paragraph{Dose-response ablation.} To control for data volume effects, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the error injection ratio (10\%, 25\%, 50\%), using seed=42 only.
43	
44	\paragraph{Recovery evaluation.} We inject errors at inference time using round-robin operator assignment across 47 test tasks and measure whether error-trained models recover better than vanilla.
```

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Structural Comparison}
4	\label{app:structural}
5	
6	\begin{table}[h]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\begin{tabular}{p{2.2cm}p{2.3cm}p{2.3cm}}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Tool-Use} \cite{paladin2025} & \textbf{Embodied} \\
12	\midrule
13	Error signal & Discrete (API error codes) & Diffuse (wrong object, wrong room) \\
14	Observability & Explicit (stack trace) & Silent (no error msg) \\
15	Recovery scope & Local (retry call) & Global (replan) \\
16	Feedback & Immediate & Delayed \\
17	Error locality & Single step & Cascading \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}
20	\caption{Structural comparison: tool-use vs.\ embodied planning. Error injection benefits from discrete, observable, locally recoverable errors.}
21	\label{tab:structural}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\section{Training Dynamics}
25	\label{app:training_dynamics}
26	
27	Figure~\ref{fig:training_curves} shows training loss curves across conditions. At $\geq$25\% injection, models achieve lower final training loss than Vanilla (TaxEI-50\%: 0.259 vs.\ Vanilla: 0.288) despite worse evaluation performance (\S\ref{sec:paradox}). At 10\% injection, training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla.
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig_app_training_curves.pdf}
32	\caption{Left: training loss curves. Right: final loss vs.\ eval accuracy---lower loss corresponds to worse performance at $\geq$25\% injection.}
33	\label{fig:training_curves}
34	\end{figure}
35	
36	\section{Per-Task Cross-Analysis}
37	\label{app:cross_analysis}
38	
39	The $\sim$6.4pp aggregate gap between TaxEI and Random at each dose point corresponds to $\approx$3 tasks on a 47-task evaluation. Cross-dose analysis reveals this is a discretization coincidence: zero tasks appear as TaxEI-penalty in all three dose points (10\%, 25\%, 50\%). The specific tasks that flip between conditions rotate across doses---9 different tasks contribute to the penalty, with no single task appearing at all three levels.
40	
41	\section{Version Discrepancy}
42	\label{app:version}
43	
44	Our evaluation pipeline evolved from v3 to v4 during development. The v3 vanilla baseline (74.5\%, seed=42) was higher due to a format mismatch. The v3$\to$v4 transition involved three changes: (1)~prompt format alignment from ChatML to tool-format, (2)~sample count increase from 4{,}176 to 4{,}358 due to improved trajectory collection, and (3)~seed handling standardization. The 12.8pp drop (74.5\%$\to$61.7\%) is attributable primarily to the format alignment. All three conditions (Vanilla, TaxEI, Random) were trained and evaluated under the same v4 pipeline, ensuring fair internal comparisons.
45

Each condition is trained with three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response ablation.} To control for data volume effects, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the error injection ratio (10\\%, 25\\%, 50\\%), using seed=42 only.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery evaluation.} We inject errors at inference time using round-robin operator assignment across 47 test tasks and measure whether error-trained models recover better than vanilla.",

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch — Round 2 审稿修复，8 项]\n\n## MF-1: Checklist framing + Title\n1. **Title** 全文改：从 \"Boundary Conditions\" → \"Hypotheses\"\n2. **Conclusion** 4 条 criteria 前加 \"We hypothesize four properties that may moderate error injection effectiveness—all require independent validation, including on ALFWorld itself:\"\n3. **Criterion (3)** 从 \"immediate feedback\" 改为 \"immediate **diagnostic** feedback\"——反馈需指示错误类型或修复方向，而非仅 binary success/failure。ALFWorld 的 \"Nothing happens\" 是 immediate 但 non-diagnostic。\n4. **Criterion (4)** 标注为 untested confound：加 \"(untested in this work; inferred from PALADIN's design)\"\n5. **Limitations** 加一句：ALFWorld provides immediate binary feedback (\"Nothing happens\") but not diagnostic feedback indicating error type or correction direction, partially resembling tool-use observability.\n\n## MF-2: Table 2 Δ 列 10% 行\n10% 行的 Δ 列改为 \"—\"（而非 -3.6pp / +2.8pp）。Caption 加脚注：10% uses single seed (seed=42); Δ not computed as baseline differs from 3-seed mean used for other rows.\n\n## MF-3: 页数压缩\n1. **Limitations** 从 Discussion 嵌入改为独立 `\\section*{Limitations}`（ACL 格式不计入页数限制）\n2. **§3.6 Training-Evaluation Paradox** 内容移至 Appendix（新建 Appendix section \"Training-Evaluation Paradox\"），正文 Discussion 中仅保留 1 句引用 Appendix\n3. 编译确认正文 ≤ 4 页\n\n## SF-1: Recovery eval 标注\nRecovery accuracy 部分加标注 \"(seed=42 only, preliminary)\"。0% detection rate 保留但加 \"(seed=42)\" 标注。\n\n## M-1: Detection rate 机制讨论\nDiscussion 增 1-2 句：The uniform 0% detection rate may reflect that SFT does not internalize taxonomy labels when they appear only as prompt prefixes—the training objective (next-token prediction) does not incentivize error classification.\n\n## M-3: BibTeX 格式\n检查 references.bib 中 author 字段，double-brace `{{Author, I.}}` 改为标准 `{Last, First and Last, First}` 格式。\n\n## Q3: PALADIN ~30pp 来源\nDiscussion 中 \"contributing an estimated ~30pp of its +57pp system-level gain\" — 如果你能确认 PALADIN 论文中有这个消融数据，保留并加引用。如果没有明确来源，改为 \"contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain\"。\n\n## 约束\n- 4 页正文限制\n- 编译确认\n- 修改完成后报告改动清单"
}

3. **Criterion (3)** 从 "immediate feedback" 改为 "immediate **diagnostic** feedback"——反馈需指示错误类型或修复方向，而非仅 binary success/failure。ALFWorld 的 "Nothing happens" 是 immediate 但 non-diagnostic。
4. **Criterion (4)** 标注为 untested confound：加 "(untested in this work; inferred from PALADIN's design)"
5. **Limitations** 加一句：ALFWorld provides immediate binary feedback ("Nothing happens") but not diagnostic feedback indicating error type or correction direction, partially resembling tool-use observability.

## MF-2: Table 2 Δ 列 10% 行
10% 行的 Δ 列改为 "—"（而非 -3.6pp / +2.8pp）。Caption 加脚注：10% uses single seed (seed=42); Δ not computed as baseline differs from 3-seed mean used for other rows.

## MF-3: 页数压缩
1. **Limitations** 从 Discussion 嵌入改为独立 `\section*{Limitations}`（ACL 格式不计入页数限制）
2. **§3.6 Training-Evaluation Paradox** 内容移至 Appendix（新建 Appendix section "Training-Evaluation Paradox"），正文 Discussion 中仅保留 1 句引用 Appendix
3. 编译确认正文 ≤ 4 页

## SF-1: Recovery eval 标注
Recovery accuracy 部分加标注 "(seed=42 only, preliminary)"。0% detection rate 保留但加 "(seed=42)" 标注。

## M-1: Detection rate 机制讨论
Discussion 增 1-2 句：The uniform 0% detection rate may reflect that SFT does not internalize taxonomy labels when they appear only as prompt prefixes—the training objective (next-token prediction) does not incentivize error classification.

## M-3: BibTeX 格式
检查 references.bib 中 author 字段，double-brace `{{Author, I.}}` 改为标准 `{Last, First and Last, First}` 格式。

## Q3: PALADIN ~30pp 来源
Discussion 中 "contributing an estimated ~30pp of its +57pp system-level gain" — 如果你能确认 PALADIN 论文中有这个消融数据，保留并加引用。如果没有明确来源，改为 "contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain"。

## 约束
- 4 页正文限制
- 编译确认
- 修改完成后报告改动清单

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}
5	We hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\sim$97\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}
8	PALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing an estimated $\sim$30pp of its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}
11	At 50\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.
12	
13	\paragraph{Limitations.}
14	We evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\%/50\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld uses a text-based interface where errors produce discrete feedback (``Nothing happens''), partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.
15	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We tested whether unpaired taxonomy-guided error injection---effective for tool-use agents---transfers to embodied planning. On ALFWorld, TaxEI provides no statistically significant improvement over clean training ($-8.6$pp, $p{=}0.054$). Two competing hypotheses explain this: embodied planning may lack the discrete, observable error structure required for injection to help, or the absence of recovery pairing (present in PALADIN) may render standalone errors unlearnable. We additionally identify a dose-dependent dual regime (3-seed confirmed at 25\%/50\%): deterministic degradation at moderate injection, chaotic seed-dependent outcomes at high injection. We also observe a per-operator local/global split (exploratory, $N{=}11$--$12$).
5	
6	Based on our results and the two competing hypotheses, we suggest that practitioners considering error injection for embodied tasks check four properties: (1)~are errors discrete and observable? (2)~can recovery be achieved locally? (3)~does the task provide immediate feedback? (4)~are errors paired with explicit recovery demonstrations? These criteria require validation in other environments.
7	
8	Future work should first test recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld to disentangle the task-structure hypothesis from recovery-pairing effects, then replicate on a second environment (e.g., WebShop) and investigate the seed-initialization $\times$ injection interaction observed at high doses.
9	
```
