```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \S\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).
5	
6	\subsection{Main Results}
7	\label{sec:main_results}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\texttt{pick\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \texttt{pick\_two\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$N$} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} \\
17	\midrule
18	Vanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\pm$3.3 \\
19	TaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\pm$8.6 \\
20	Random  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\pm$7.0 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \%). TaxEI vs.\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}
24	\label{tab:main_results}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	At full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\% (SD${\approx}$12pp).
28	
29	\subsection{Dose-Response}
30	\label{sec:dose_response}
31	
32	To control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\% and 50\% reveal a dual regime.
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\small
37	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
38	\toprule
39	\textbf{Dose} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{TaxEI}} \\
42	10\%$^\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\
43	25\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\
44	50\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Random}} \\
47	10\%$^\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\
48	25\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\
49	50\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\Delta$: mean change vs.\ vanilla (65.3$\pm$3.3). $^\dagger$Single seed. At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\%, variance explodes (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\%.}
53	\label{tab:dose_response}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}
59	\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\%$\to$50\%.}
60	\label{fig:dose_response}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\sim$47\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\% TaxEI mean (56.0\%) exceeds 25\% mean (47.5\%). At 10\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\%, within vanilla seed range.
64	
65	\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}
66	\label{sec:recovery}
67	
68	We test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\% recovery (vs.\ Vanilla 61.7\%, Random 63.8\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.
69	
70	\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}
71	\label{sec:operator_split}
72	
73	Figure~\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}
78	\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\pm1$ task $\approx$ $\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}
79	\label{fig:operator_gradient}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}
83	\label{sec:paradox}
84	
85	At $\geq$25\% injection, models can achieve \emph{lower} training loss but \emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\% (vs.\ 61.7\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\% despite similar training dynamics.
86	
```

At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\\%$\\to$50\\%.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe tested whether unpaired taxonomy-guided error injection---effective for tool-use agents---transfers to embodied planning.     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}\nWe hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}\nPALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing an estimated $\\sim$30pp of its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}\nAt 50\\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld uses a text-based interface where errors produce discrete feedback (``Nothing happens''), partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & $-$3.6 \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & $+$2.8 \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\\%$\\to$50\\%.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",

These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & --- \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & --- \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed (seed=42); $\\Delta$ omitted as baseline differs from the 3-seed mean used for other rows. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\\%$\\to$50\\%.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.",
    "newString": "",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \\textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \\textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \\textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \\S\\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\\texttt{pick\\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \\texttt{pick\\_two\\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$N$} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\\pm$3.3 \\\\\nTaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\\pm$8.6 \\\\\nRandom  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\\pm$7.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \\%). TaxEI vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\\% (SD${\\approx}$12pp).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nTo control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\\% and 50\\% reveal a dual regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dose} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s0} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean$\\pm$SD} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{TaxEI}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & --- \\\\\n25\\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\\\\n50\\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Random}} \\\\\n10\\%$^\\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & --- \\\\\n25\\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\\\\n50\\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\\Delta$: mean change vs.\\ vanilla (65.3$\\pm$3.3). $^\\dagger$Single seed (seed=42); $\\Delta$ omitted as baseline differs from the 3-seed mean used for other rows. At 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\\%, variance explodes (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\\%$\\to$50\\%.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 25\\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\\sim$47\\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\\% TaxEI mean (56.0\\%) exceeds 25\\% mean (47.5\\%). At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42 only, preliminary). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate (seed=42) suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Training-Evaluation Paradox}\n\\label{sec:paradox}\n\nAt $\\geq$25\\% injection, models can achieve \\emph{lower} training loss but \\emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \\cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "6cbeca39-13e2-4961-9143-def33e0679db",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld uses a text-based interface where errors produce discrete feedback (``Nothing happens''), partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.",
    "newString": "We additionally observe a training-evaluation paradox at $\\geq$25\\% injection, where models achieve lower training loss but worse evaluation performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:paradox}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}\nWe hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}\nPALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}\nAt 50\\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds).     "newString": "This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection rate.}\nThe uniform 0\\% detection rate (\\S\\ref{sec:recovery}) may reflect that SFT does not internalize taxonomy labels when they appear only as prompt prefixes---the training objective (next-token prediction) does not incentivize error classification, so models learn trajectory patterns rather than error type distinctions.\n\nWe additionally observe",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}\nWe hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \\cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\\sim$97\\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}\nPALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}\nAt 50\\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\\% to 70.2\\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.\n\nWe additionally observe a training-evaluation paradox at $\\geq$25\\% injection, where models achieve lower training loss but worse evaluation performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:paradox}).\n",

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Structural Comparison}
4	\label{app:structural}
5	
6	\begin{table}[h]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\begin{tabular}{p{2.2cm}p{2.3cm}p{2.3cm}}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Tool-Use} \cite{paladin2025} & \textbf{Embodied} \\
12	\midrule
13	Error signal & Discrete (API error codes) & Diffuse (wrong object, wrong room) \\
14	Observability & Explicit (stack trace) & Silent (no error msg) \\
15	Recovery scope & Local (retry call) & Global (replan) \\
16	Feedback & Immediate & Delayed \\
17	Error locality & Single step & Cascading \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}
20	\caption{Structural comparison: tool-use vs.\ embodied planning. Error injection benefits from discrete, observable, locally recoverable errors.}
21	\label{tab:structural}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\section{Training Dynamics}
25	\label{app:training_dynamics}
26	
27	Figure~\ref{fig:training_curves} shows training loss curves across conditions. At $\geq$25\% injection, models achieve lower final training loss than Vanilla (TaxEI-50\%: 0.259 vs.\ Vanilla: 0.288) despite worse evaluation performance (\S\ref{sec:paradox}). At 10\% injection, training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla.
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig_app_training_curves.pdf}
32	\caption{Left: training loss curves. Right: final loss vs.\ eval accuracy---lower loss corresponds to worse performance at $\geq$25\% injection.}
33	\label{fig:training_curves}
34	\end{figure}
35	
36	\section{Per-Task Cross-Analysis}
37	\label{app:cross_analysis}
38	
39	For seed=42, the $\sim$6.4pp aggregate gap between TaxEI and Random at each dose point corresponds to $\approx$3 tasks on a 47-task evaluation. This consistency does not hold across seeds: at 25\% the 3-seed mean gap is 8.5pp, at 50\% it is 2.1pp. Cross-dose analysis reveals this is a discretization coincidence: zero tasks appear as TaxEI-penalty in all three dose points (10\%, 25\%, 50\%). The specific tasks that flip between conditions rotate across doses---9 different tasks contribute to the penalty, with no single task appearing at all three levels.
40	
41	\section{Version Discrepancy}
42	\label{app:version}
43	
44	Our evaluation pipeline evolved from v3 to v4 during development. The v3 vanilla baseline (74.5\%, seed=42) was higher due to a format mismatch. The v3$\to$v4 transition involved three changes: (1)~prompt format alignment from ChatML to tool-format, (2)~sample count increase from 4{,}176 to 4{,}358 due to improved trajectory collection, and (3)~seed handling standardization. The 12.8pp drop (74.5\%$\to$61.7\%) is attributable primarily to the format alignment. All three conditions (Vanilla, TaxEI, Random) were trained and evaluated under the same v4 pipeline, ensuring fair internal comparisons.
45	
```

For seed=42, TaxEI-50\\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\\% (vs.\\ 61.7\\%). The extreme seed variance at 50\\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\\% despite similar training dynamics.\n\n\\section{Per-Task Cross-Analysis}"
}

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/tax_error_inject_agent/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
  "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld provides immediate binary feedback (``Nothing happens'') but not diagnostic feedback indicating error type or correction direction, partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.\n"
}
```

</details>

    "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nWe evaluate on a single environment (ALFWorld, 47 tasks) with one base model. Three seeds provide limited statistical power; the 10\\% dose-response uses a single seed (25\\%/50\\% confirmed across 3 seeds). TaxEI/Random use $2.1\\times$ more training data than Vanilla; the fixed-size ablation controls for this. Per-operator analysis ($N{=}11$--$12$) is exploratory. ALFWorld provides immediate binary feedback (``Nothing happens'') but not diagnostic feedback indicating error type or correction direction, partially resembling tool-use error observability. Vision-based embodied tasks with continuous state spaces may exhibit different error injection dynamics. We compare only error injection variants; other augmentation strategies (e.g., paraphrasing, trajectory diversification) were not tested, leaving open whether the negative result generalizes to all off-distribution augmentation at this data scale.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [],

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}
5	We hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\sim$97\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}
8	PALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}
11	At 50\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.
12	
13	\paragraph{Detection rate.}
14	The uniform 0\% detection rate (\S\ref{sec:recovery}) may reflect that SFT does not internalize taxonomy labels when they appear only as prompt prefixes---the training objective (next-token prediction) does not incentivize error classification, so models learn trajectory patterns rather than error type distinctions.
15	
16	We additionally observe a training-evaluation paradox at $\geq$25\% injection, where models achieve lower training loss but worse evaluation performance (Appendix~\ref{app:paradox}).
17	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \S\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).
5	
6	\subsection{Main Results}
7	\label{sec:main_results}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\texttt{pick\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \texttt{pick\_two\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$N$} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} \\
17	\midrule
18	Vanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\pm$3.3 \\
19	TaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\pm$8.6 \\
20	Random  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\pm$7.0 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \%). TaxEI vs.\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}
24	\label{tab:main_results}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	At full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\% (SD${\approx}$12pp).
28	
29	\subsection{Dose-Response}
30	\label{sec:dose_response}
31	
32	To control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\% and 50\% reveal a dual regime.
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\small
37	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
38	\toprule
39	\textbf{Dose} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{TaxEI}} \\
42	10\%$^\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & --- \\
43	25\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\
44	50\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Random}} \\
47	10\%$^\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & --- \\
48	25\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\
49	50\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\Delta$: mean change vs.\ vanilla (65.3$\pm$3.3). $^\dagger$Single seed (seed=42); $\Delta$ omitted as baseline differs from the 3-seed mean used for other rows. At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\%, variance explodes (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\%.}
53	\label{tab:dose_response}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}
59	\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\%$\to$50\%.}
60	\label{fig:dose_response}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\sim$47\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\% TaxEI mean (56.0\%) exceeds 25\% mean (47.5\%). At 10\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\%, within vanilla seed range.
64	
65	\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}
66	\label{sec:recovery}
67	
68	We test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42 only, preliminary). TaxEI achieves 59.6\% recovery (vs.\ Vanilla 61.7\%, Random 63.8\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\% detection rate (seed=42) suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.
69	
70	\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}
71	\label{sec:operator_split}
72	
73	Figure~\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}
78	\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\pm1$ task $\approx$ $\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}
79	\label{fig:operator_gradient}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	
```

At 10\\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\\%, within vanilla seed range.\n\n\\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:recovery}\n\nWe test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42 only, preliminary). TaxEI achieves 59.6\\% recovery (vs.\\ Vanilla 61.7\\%, Random 63.8\\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\\% detection rate (seed=42) suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}\n\\label{sec:operator_split}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\\pm1$ task $\\approx$ $\\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}\n\\label{fig:operator_gradient}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",
      "numLines": 82,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 82
    }
  },

当前 Discussion 只有 3 个 \paragraph（structural hypothesis, recovery-pairing confound, training lottery）+ Limitations 已分离。增加内容：
- Structural hypothesis 段落可以更详细展开 Table 3 (Appendix) 中的维度对比
- Training lottery 段落可以增加对实践者的具体 guidance：单 seed 评估不可靠、至少 3 seeds、高注入比例需要格外谨慎
- 新增 1 段讨论 0% detection rate 的机制：SFT 的 next-token prediction 目标不激励 error classification，taxonomy labels 作为 prompt prefix 未被内化（Round 2 M-1 要求的，之前已加但可以更充实）

### 3. Related Work 适当扩展
补充 2 个引用（reviewer 建议但标注为非必须）：
- LATS (Zhou et al., 2024): Language Agent Tree Search, ALFWorld 上有竞争力结果
- FireAct (Chen et al., 2023): fine-tuning agents with multiple reasoning traces
各 1 句即可。

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate three training conditions on ALFWorld's 47 unseen test tasks \cite{shridhar2021alfworld}: \textbf{Vanilla} (4{,}358 clean demonstrations), \textbf{TaxEI} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 taxonomy-tagged error trajectories = 9{,}029), and \textbf{Random} (4{,}358 clean + 4{,}671 generic-labeled errors = 9{,}029). Training details are in \S\ref{sec:training_setup}. We report results across three seeds (42, 0, 123).
5	
6	\subsection{Main Results}
7	\label{sec:main_results}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} shows three-seed evaluation results. TaxEI degrades directionally ($-8.6$pp vs.\ Vanilla), but the difference is not statistically significant (permutation test, $p{=}0.054$, $N{=}10{,}000$). No TaxEI seed exceeds the Vanilla mean. Random injection shows minimal degradation ($-2.2$pp, $p{>}0.3$). The TaxEI--Random gap is driven by two of six task types (\texttt{pick\_cool}: $-19.0$pp; \texttt{pick\_two\_obj}: $-14.3$pp).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$N$} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} \\
17	\midrule
18	Vanilla & 4{,}358 & 61.7 & 68.1 & 66.0 & 65.3$\pm$3.3 \\
19	TaxEI   & 9{,}029 & 61.7 & 46.8 & 61.7 & 56.7$\pm$8.6 \\
20	Random  & 9{,}029 & 66.0 & 55.3 & 68.1 & 63.1$\pm$7.0 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\caption{Three-seed clean evaluation on ALFWorld (47 tasks, accuracy \%). TaxEI vs.\ Vanilla: $p{=}0.054$; TaxEI vs.\ Random: $p{=}0.170$; Random vs.\ Vanilla: $p{>}0.3$ (pooled permutation, 10K). Note: TaxEI/Random use 9{,}029 samples ($2.1\times$ vanilla). The dose-response ablation (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}) controls for data volume at a fixed 4{,}358 total.}
24	\label{tab:main_results}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	At full training scale, standard deviations follow Vanilla (3.3pp) $<$ Random (7.0pp) $<$ TaxEI (8.6pp), though not statistically confirmed at $n{=}3$. The dose-response ablation (\S\ref{sec:dose_response}) reveals this variance is dose-dependent: deterministic at 25\% (SD${=}$1.2pp) but chaotic at 50\% (SD${\approx}$12pp).
28	
29	\subsection{Dose-Response}
30	\label{sec:dose_response}
31	
32	To control for data volume, we fix total training size at 4{,}358 and vary the injection ratio (Table~\ref{tab:dose_response}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}). Multi-seed results at 25\% and 50\% reveal a dual regime.
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\small
37	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
38	\toprule
39	\textbf{Dose} & \textbf{s42} & \textbf{s0} & \textbf{s123} & \textbf{Mean$\pm$SD} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{TaxEI}} \\
42	10\%$^\dagger$ & 61.7 & --- & --- & 61.7 & --- \\
43	25\%  & 48.9 & 46.8 & 46.8 & 47.5$\pm$1.2 & $-$17.8 \\
44	50\%  & 51.1 & 70.2 & 46.8 & 56.0$\pm$12.4 & $-$9.3 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{Random}} \\
47	10\%$^\dagger$ & 68.1 & --- & --- & 68.1 & --- \\
48	25\%  & 55.3 & 61.7 & 51.1 & 56.0$\pm$5.3 & $-$9.3 \\
49	50\%  & 57.4 & 46.8 & 70.2 & 58.1$\pm$11.7 & $-$7.2 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}
52	\caption{Dose-response ablation (fixed 4{,}358 total). $\Delta$: mean change vs.\ vanilla (65.3$\pm$3.3). $^\dagger$Single seed (seed=42); $\Delta$ omitted as baseline differs from the 3-seed mean used for other rows. At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically (SD${=}$1.2pp). At 50\%, variance explodes (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds spanning 46.8--70.2\%.}
53	\label{tab:dose_response}
54	\end{table}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_dose_response.pdf}
59	\caption{Dose-response (seed=42 only). 3-seed data in Table~\ref{tab:dose_response} reveals non-monotonic dose-response with regime bifurcation at 25\%$\to$50\%.}
60	\label{fig:dose_response}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	At 25\%, TaxEI degrades deterministically: all three seeds converge to $\sim$47\% ($-17.8$pp, SD${=}$1.2pp). Random also degrades ($-9.3$pp, SD${=}$5.3pp). At 50\%, both methods exhibit extreme seed sensitivity (SD${\approx}$12pp), with seeds ranging from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The dose-response is non-monotonic: 50\% TaxEI mean (56.0\%) exceeds 25\% mean (47.5\%). At 10\% (single seed), TaxEI matches Vanilla exactly; Random achieves 68.1\%, within vanilla seed range.
64	
65	\subsection{Recovery Evaluation}
66	\label{sec:recovery}
67	
68	We test whether error-trained models recover from injected errors at inference time (round-robin operator injection, 47 tasks, seed=42 only, preliminary). TaxEI achieves 59.6\% recovery (vs.\ Vanilla 61.7\%, Random 63.8\%)---no statistically significant improvement. The uniform 0\% detection rate (seed=42) suggests taxonomy labels were not internalized during SFT---models learned trajectory patterns rather than error classification.
69	
70	\subsection{Per-Operator Local/Global Split}
71	\label{sec:operator_split}
72	
73	Figure~\ref{fig:operator_gradient} decomposes recovery by operator (seed=42, $N{=}11$--$12$). Local operators (C4 observation masking: 7/11 vs.\ 6/11, $+9.1$pp; P1 step swap: 7/12 vs.\ 7/12, $0.0$pp) are neutral-to-beneficial; global operators (C1 context truncation: 8/12 vs.\ 9/12, $-8.3$pp; P2 sub-goal deletion: 6/12 vs.\ 7/12, $-8.3$pp) are harmful. Local errors leave the causal structure intact; global errors propagate downstream. These results are exploratory ($N{=}11$--$12$).
74

77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_operator_gradient.pdf}
78	\caption{Per-operator recovery delta (TaxEI $-$ Vanilla, seed=42). $N{=}11$--$12$; $\pm1$ task $\approx$ $\pm$8--9pp. Exploratory.}
79	\label{fig:operator_gradient}
80	\end{figure}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RQA4Vpbj6NGfvz9ptHiixF`</summary>

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Structural Comparison}
4	\label{app:structural}
5	
6	\begin{table}[h]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\begin{tabular}{p{2.2cm}p{2.3cm}p{2.3cm}}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Tool-Use} \cite{paladin2025} & \textbf{Embodied} \\
12	\midrule
13	Error signal & Discrete (API error codes) & Diffuse (wrong object, wrong room) \\
14	Observability & Explicit (stack trace) & Silent (no error msg) \\
15	Recovery scope & Local (retry call) & Global (replan) \\
16	Feedback & Immediate & Delayed \\
17	Error locality & Single step & Cascading \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}
20	\caption{Structural comparison: tool-use vs.\ embodied planning. Error injection benefits from discrete, observable, locally recoverable errors.}
21	\label{tab:structural}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\section{Training Dynamics}
25	\label{app:training_dynamics}
26	
27	Figure~\ref{fig:training_curves} shows training loss curves across conditions. At $\geq$25\% injection, models achieve lower final training loss than Vanilla (TaxEI-50\%: 0.259 vs.\ Vanilla: 0.288) despite worse evaluation performance (\S\ref{app:paradox}). At 10\% injection, training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla.
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig_app_training_curves.pdf}
32	\caption{Left: training loss curves. Right: final loss vs.\ eval accuracy---lower loss corresponds to worse performance at $\geq$25\% injection.}
33	\label{fig:training_curves}
34	\end{figure}
35	
36	\section{Training-Evaluation Paradox}
37	\label{app:paradox}
38	
39	At $\geq$25\% injection, models can achieve \emph{lower} training loss but \emph{worse} evaluation performance. For seed=42, TaxEI-50\% reaches final loss 0.259 (vs.\ Vanilla 0.288) yet evaluates at 51.1\% (vs.\ 61.7\%). This paradox does not hold at 10\%, where training loss (0.308) remains above Vanilla. Two explanations are plausible: memorization of noise \cite{arpit2017memorization,zhou2023lima} and distribution shift at high injection ratios. The extreme seed variance at 50\% suggests this paradox is seed-dependent: TaxEI-50\% seed=0 achieves 70.2\% despite similar training dynamics.
40	
41	\section{Per-Task Cross-Analysis}
42	\label{app:cross_analysis}
43	
44	For seed=42, the $\sim$6.4pp aggregate gap between TaxEI and Random at each dose point corresponds to $\approx$3 tasks on a 47-task evaluation. This consistency does not hold across seeds: at 25\% the 3-seed mean gap is 8.5pp, at 50\% it is 2.1pp. Cross-dose analysis reveals this is a discretization coincidence: zero tasks appear as TaxEI-penalty in all three dose points (10\%, 25\%, 50\%). The specific tasks that flip between conditions rotate across doses---9 different tasks contribute to the penalty, with no single task appearing at all three levels.
45	
46	\section{Version Discrepancy}
47	\label{app:version}
48	
49	Our evaluation pipeline evolved from v3 to v4 during development. The v3 vanilla baseline (74.5\%, seed=42) was higher due to a format mismatch. The v3$\to$v4 transition involved three changes: (1)~prompt format alignment from ChatML to tool-format, (2)~sample count increase from 4{,}176 to 4{,}358 due to improved trajectory collection, and (3)~seed handling standardization. The 12.8pp drop (74.5\%$\to$61.7\%) is attributable primarily to the format alignment. All three conditions (Vanilla, TaxEI, Random) were trained and evaluated under the same v4 pipeline, ensuring fair internal comparisons.
50

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Structural hypothesis.}
5	We hypothesize that the transfer failure stems from structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning (Table~\ref{tab:structural} in Appendix). In tool-use, errors produce discrete signals (API error codes) mapping to specific recovery actions; taxonomy labels encode this mapping. In embodied planning, errors propagate silently---picking the wrong object produces no error message, and recovery requires replanning from an unknown state. Reflexion \cite{shinn2023reflexion} achieves $\sim$97\% on ALFWorld through inference-time failure reflection, suggesting that inference-time self-correction succeeds where training-time injection fails in embodied planning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Recovery-pairing confound.}
8	PALADIN pairs each error with an explicit recovery demonstration via retrieval augmentation, contributing substantially to its +57pp system-level gain. Our TaxEI injects standalone errors without paired recoveries. We cannot distinguish between these two hypotheses with the current design: the negative result may stem from (a)~missing recovery pairing, or (b)~structural differences between tool-use and embodied planning, or both. Recovery-paired injection on ALFWorld is needed to disentangle these factors.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training lottery at high injection.}
11	At 50\% injection, individual seeds range from 46.8\% to 70.2\%---the latter exceeding all vanilla seeds. The 70.2\% anomaly occurs in both TaxEI (seed=0) and Random (seed=123), suggesting a generic high-dose phenomenon independent of taxonomy labeling. This cross-condition coincidence may reflect a seed-initialization $\times$ injection interaction warranting further investigation.
12	
13	\paragraph{Detection rate.}
14	The uniform 0\% detection rate (\S\ref{sec:recovery}) may reflect that SFT does not internalize taxonomy labels when they appear only as prompt prefixes---the training objective (next-token prediction) does not incentivize error classification, so models learn trajectory patterns rather than error type distinctions.
15	
16	We additionally observe a training-evaluation paradox at $\geq$25\% injection, where models achieve lower training loss but worse evaluation performance (Appendix~\ref{app:paradox}).
17	
```
